Given H100's high memory bandwidth (3.35 TB/s), the kernel is likely memory-bound. So the main optimization is to maximize memory throughput through vectorized loads/stores and appropriate block size. Let's analyze the kernels: 1. `static_scaled_int8_quant_kernel`: reads fp16 input, writes int8 output. No shared memory needed. Operation: `out = clamp(round(in / scale))`. This is elementwise. To maximize throughput, we want each thread to process many elements with large vectorized loads/stores. On H100, 128-bit (16 bytes) loads per thread are good. Since the block size can be up to 1024, but the original uses `min(hidden_size, 256)`. For H100, we might want larger blocks (e.g., 512 or 1024) to increase occupancy, but given it's memory-bound, occupancy isn't the bottleneck; memory bandwidth is. However, more threads can help saturate HBM. H100 can have up to 2048 threads per SM. With block size 256 and many tokens (M=16), total threads = 16*256 = 4096, which may not fully saturate the GPU if hidden_size is large. Actually hidden_size = K = 4096. So each row has 4096 elements. With block size 256, each thread processes 16 elements. That's fine. With block size 512, each thread processes 8 elements. With block size 1024, each thread processes 4 elements. Any of these can be fine. For vectorization, we can have each thread process elements in chunks of 16 bytes (8 fp16 inputs, 16 int8 outputs). So if block size is 256, each thread does 4096/256 = 16 elements. That's 2 chunks of 8 fp16 elements. We can process 8 elements per iteration. 2. `dynamic_scaled_int8_quant_kernel`: reads fp16 input to compute absmax per row (block). This requires a block reduction. Then writes int8 output. The first pass is read-only to compute absmax, second pass reads again and writes. Since the input is not kept in shared memory (unless we tile), each element is read twice. We can tile into shared memory to avoid double-reading from global memory. For a row of 4096 fp16 elements, that's 8KB per row. With block size 256, we could load a chunk into shared memory, compute absmax, then reuse it for quantization. But shared memory is fast but the extra write might not be worth it if we can just read twice from global memory, since H100 has high bandwidth. However, reading twice means 2x the HBM traffic. If we can cache in shared memory, we reduce HBM reads by half. Since the kernel is memory-bound, halving HBM reads could almost double performance. But can we fit the row in shared memory? 4096 fp16 = 8192 bytes. That's well within the 227KB per block limit. So each block could load the entire row into shared memory. But if multiple blocks handle the same row? In the original, each block handles one token (one row). So blockIdx.x = token_idx. So one block per row. Thus the block can load the entire row into shared memory (or a chunk). Since hidden_size = 4096, block size 256, each thread loads 16 elements. We can allocate shared memory of size `blockDim.x * vector_size * sizeof(half)`? Or just have each thread load its elements and keep them in registers while doing reduction? But reduction needs to communicate max across threads. We can compute thread-local max, then block reduce. After that, we need the values again for the second pass. If we keep them in registers, we don't need shared memory or re-read from global memory. Each thread processes `hidden_size / blockDim.x` elements. For 4096/256 = 16 elements per thread. We can keep 16 fp16 values in registers (16 * 2 bytes = 32 bytes). That's trivial. So the optimal approach: each thread reads its 16 elements, computes local max, stores the 16 values in a register array (or straight away compute and store). Then after block reduction, each thread reads its register array again and writes quantized output. This avoids both shared memory and double global memory reads. In the original code, `vectorize_read_with_alignment` reads and then `vectorize_with_alignment` reads again. That causes two global memory reads. By manually fusing, we can read once. But the original code is generic and uses lambdas. For our optimized kernel, we can write a specialized version for fp16 with manual unrolling. Similarly, the `static_scaled_int8_azp_quant_kernel` and `dynamic_scaled_int8_azp_quant_kernel` have similar patterns. We need to support both static and dynamic, with optional azp. The correctness check doesn't care about block size. Now, for the dynamic kernel, we can avoid CUB and use warp shuffles for reduction, which is often faster. Since we need the max across all threads, we can do: ```cpp // compute thread_max float thread_max = 0.f; for (int i = tid; i < hidden_size; i += blockDim.x) { // load 8 elements? } // warp reduce for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, thread_max, offset)); } // block reduce using shared memory __shared__ float warp_max[32]; // max 32 warps per block (for 1024 threads) if (lane == 0) warp_max[warp_id] = thread_max; __syncthreads(); if (warp_id == 0) { float val = (lane < num_warps) ? warp_max[lane] : 0.f; for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); } if (lane == 0) absmax = val; } __syncthreads(); ``` This avoids CUB and can be faster. After reduction, each thread processes its elements again (from registers or re-read from global mem). To avoid re-reading, we can load elements into a local array. Now, let's write the inline CUDA module. We need to define `dynamic_scaled_int8_quant`. For H100, using 512 threads per block and `__half2` loads with `__ldg`. Let me craft the code carefully. CUDA source: ```cuda #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <c10/cuda/CUDAGuard.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> static inline __device__ int8_t float_to_int8_rn(float x) { uint32_t dst; asm volatile("cvt.rni.sat.s8.f32 %0, %1;" : "=r"(dst) : "f"(x)); return reinterpret_cast<const int8_t&>(dst); } __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); } return val; } template <typename scalar_t> __global__ void dynamic_scaled_int8_quant_kernel( const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, int8_t* __restrict__ output, float* __restrict__ scale_out, const int hidden_size) { const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; const scalar_t* row_in = input + static_cast<int64_t>(token_idx) * hidden_size; int8_t* row_out = output + static_cast<int64_t>(token_idx) * hidden_size; float thread_max = 0.0f; if constexpr (std::is_same<scalar_t, at::Half>::value) { // Process in __half2 chunks (4 bytes = 2 fp16) const int h2_per_row = hidden_size >> 1; const __half2* h2_in = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(row_in); #pragma unroll 4 for (int i = tid; i < h2_per_row; i += blockDim.x) { __half2 h2 = __ldg(&h2_in[i]); float2 f2 = __half22float2(h2); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(f2.x)); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(f2.y)); } // Handle odd hidden_size if (hidden_size & 1) { float v = fabsf(static_cast<float>(row_in[hidden_size - 1])); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, v); } } else { for (int i = tid; i < hidden_size; i += blockDim.x) { float v = fabsf(static_cast<float>(row_in[i])); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, v); } } // Block reduction using warp shuffles thread_max = warp_reduce_max(thread_max); const int warp_id = tid >> 5; const int lane_id = tid & 31; __shared__ float smem[32]; // at most 32 warps for blockDim.x <= 1024 if (lane_id == 0) { smem[warp_id] = thread_max; } __syncthreads(); if (warp_id == 0) { thread_max = (lane_id < (blockDim.x >> 5)) ? smem[lane_id] : 0.0f; thread_max = warp_reduce_max(thread_max); } __shared__ float absmax; if (tid == 0) { absmax = thread_max; scale_out[token_idx] = absmax / 127.0f; } __syncthreads(); float inv_s = (absmax == 0.0f) ? 0.0f : 127.0f / absmax; if constexpr (std::is_same<scalar_t, at::Half>::value) { const int h2_per_row = hidden_size >> 1; const __half2* h2_in = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(row_in); #pragma unroll 4 for (int i = tid; i < h2_per_row; i += blockDim.x) { __half2 h2 = __ldg(&h2_in[i]); float2 f2 = __half22float2(h2); float v0 = f2.x * inv_s; float v1 = f2.y * inv_s; row_out[i * 2] = float_to_int8_rn(v0); row_out[i * 2 + 1] = float_to_int8_rn(v1); } if ((hidden_size & 1) && tid == 0) { float v = static_cast<float>(row_in[hidden_size - 1]) * inv_s; row_out[hidden_size - 1] = float_to_int8_rn(v); } } else { for (int i = tid; i < hidden_size; i += blockDim.x) { float v = static_cast<float>(row_in[i]) * inv_s; row_out[i] = float_to_int8_rn(v); } } } void dynamic_scaled_int8_quant(torch::Tensor out, torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor scales) { TORCH_CHECK(input.is_contiguous()); TORCH_CHECK(out.is_contiguous()); TORCH_CHECK(scales.is_contiguous()); int hidden_size = input.size(-1); int num_tokens = input.numel() / hidden_size; dim3 grid(num_tokens); // Use 512 threads for H100 to better utilize memory bandwidth per block dim3 block(512); const at::cuda::OptionalCUDAGuard device_guard(device_of(input)); const cudaStream_t stream = at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES_AND2( at::ScalarType::Half, at::ScalarType::BFloat16, input.scalar_type(), "dynamic_scaled_int8_quant", [&] { dynamic_scaled_int8_quant_kernel<scalar_t> <<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( input.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), out.data_ptr<int8_t>(), scales.data_ptr<float>(), hidden_size); }); } ``` Wait, `AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES_AND2` is available but we need to ensure we include the right headers. In `torch/extension.h`, this macro is available. However, using `if constexpr` with `std::is_same` requires C++17. `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` defaults to C++14? Actually, PyTorch extension uses C++17 by default on newer versions. We can pass extra flags to be safe. Actually, `load_inline` might use `-std=c++17` by default? We can pass `extra_cuda_cflags=['-std=c++17', '-O3']`. But to be safe, we can avoid `if constexpr` and use template specialization. Also, `__ldg` on `__half2*` requires the pointer to be const __restrict__. Our `row_in` is const __restrict__ scalar_t*. When we cast to `const __half2*`, the compiler should see it's const. But `__ldg` takes a pointer; for types, we can call `__ldg(&ptr[i])` where ptr is `const __half2* __restrict__`. We need to ensure the cast pointer is also restrict. Actually, we can do: ```cuda const __half2* __restrict__ h2_in = reinterpret_cast<const __half2* __restrict__>(row_in); ``` But `reinterpret_cast` doesn't transfer `__restrict__` easily. We can do: ```cuda const __half2* h2_in = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(row_in); ``` The compiler knows `row_in` is restrict, but the alias through cast might break restrict semantics. In practice, it's fine. Alternatively, we can avoid `__ldg` and rely on the compiler. With `-O3` and `__restrict__`, the compiler generates `LDG.E` (load global) automatically. So explicit `__ldg` might not be necessary and can be omitted to avoid restrict issues. However, for cached loads, `__ldg` uses read-only cache (L1) on Ampere/Hopper. Actually, on modern GPUs, regular loads with const __restrict__ might already use the same path. I'll keep `__ldg` but be careful. Actually, there is an issue: `__ldg` is overloaded for built-in types like `int`, `float`, `__half2`, etc. It takes a pointer to const. So `const __half2* ptr; __ldg(ptr + i);` should work. Let me refine the kernel to use explicit template specialization for `at::Half` path. ```cuda template <typename scalar_t> __device__ __forceinline__ float absmax_reduce(const scalar_t* row_in, int hidden_size, int tid, int blockDim_x) { float thread_max = 0.0f; for (int i = tid; i < hidden_size; i += blockDim_x) { thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(static_cast<float>(row_in[i]))); } return thread_max; } template <> __device__ __forceinline__ float absmax_reduce<at::Half>(const at::Half* row_in, int hidden_size, int tid, int blockDim_x) { float thread_max = 0.0f; const int h2_per_row = hidden_size >> 1; const __half2* h2_in = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(row_in); #pragma unroll 4 for (int i = tid; i < h2_per_row; i += blockDim_x) { __half2 h2 = __ldg(h2_in + i); float2 f2 = __half22float2(h2); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(f2.x)); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(f2.y)); } if (hidden_size & 1) { thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, fabsf(static_cast<float>(row_in[hidden_size - 1]))); } return thread_max; } ``` Similarly for the writeback. For hidden_size=4096, each thread processes `h2_count / block_size = 2048 / 512 = 4` half2s. So each thread loads 4 `__half2` = 8 fp16 values = 16 bytes. 512 threads * 16 bytes = 8192 bytes per block, which matches one row exactly. With `#pragma unroll 4`, the loop has exactly one iteration, so it's fully unrolled. This is optimal. For correctness, we need to ensure output matches exactly. The original kernel used `vectorize_with_alignment<16>` which likely processes 16 bytes per thread. The math is: `float_to_int8_rn(static_cast<float>(src) * inv_s)`. The `inv_s` is computed as `127.f / absmax`. Our kernel computes the same `inv_s`. However, there might be slight differences in how `absmax` is reduced (order of operations). The issue is that load_inline builds a main.cpp that tries to wrap `dynamic_scaled_int8_quant` but it's only defined in the .cu file without a header. I need to put a declaration of the function in `cpp_sources`, or just put everything in `cpp_sources` and use `cuda_sources` as well, but the function needs to be declared in a way visible to the binding code. The simplest approach: add a forward declaration in `cpp_sources`, then the implementation in `cuda_sources`. Let me look at how load_inline works - it generates main.cpp that includes the cpp_sources and binds functions. The cuda_sources are compiled separately. To expose CUDA functions to the binding code, I need to declare them in cpp_sources with `extern`. Wait, actually load_inline concatenates cpp_sources and then generates bindings. The issue is that the binding code is in the C++ compilation unit but the function is defined in the CUDA compilation unit. This typically requires `extern` declaration. Actually, I think load_inline is supposed to handle this if the function is declared in cpp_sources. But the binding code is generated after cpp_sources. So I should put HEADERS/forward declarations in cpp_sources, or better yet, put everything in the cpp_sources with the `TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME` macro and avoid load_inline's separate compilation. Actually, `fmaxf` is associative in the sense that the exact mathematical max is the same regardless of order (except for NaN handling, but we don't have NaNs). And `fmaxf(a, b)` for positive numbers is exact. So the order shouldn't matter. However, `fabsf` followed by `fmaxf` - the maximum absolute value is unique and exact in float for positive finite numbers. Since we take `fabsf` first, all values are non-negative, and `fmaxf` computes the exact max. So the reduction order doesn't matter for correctness of the absmax. Actually, let me re-examine my correctness failures. Is there any chance the failure is because of pybind11 argument ordering or because `load_inline` expects different function signature? Looking at load_inline docs: ```python load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources, functions, extra_cflags, extra_cuda_cflags, extra_ldflags, extra_include_paths, build_directory, verbose, with_cuda, is_python_module, with_pytorch_error_handling, keep_intermediates) ``` The `functions` parameter tells pybind11 which functions to bind. If I have `void f(torch::Tensor a, torch::Tensor b)`, pybind11 generates a binding that takes two Python tensors. Wait, in my code: ```cpp void dynamic_scaled_int8_quant(torch::Tensor out, torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor scales) ``` This takes three arguments by value. In pybind11, this should bind to a Python function taking three positional arguments. When I call `mod.dynamic_scaled_int8_quant(out, x, scales)` in Python, it should pass: - `out` to the first parameter `out` - `x` to the second parameter `input` - `scales` to the third parameter `scales` This is correct. But here's a subtle issue: the original C++ function takes `const` and reference qualifiers: ```cpp void dynamic_scaled_int8_quant( torch::Tensor& out, // non-const ref torch::Tensor const& input, // const ref torch::Tensor& scales, // non-const ref std::optional<torch::Tensor> const& azp) ``` My function signature doesn't have references or const. In pybind11, this shouldn't matter for the binding because pybind11 handles torch::Tensor copying. Let me make sure I'm not accidentally transposing the dimensions or something in my kernel launch. In my code: ```cpp int hidden_size = input.size(-1); int num_tokens = input.numel() / hidden_size; dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(std::min(hidden_size, 256)); ``` For input shape [16, 4096]: - hidden_size = 4096 - num_tokens = 16 * 4096 / 4096 = 16 - grid = (16) - block = (256) In the kernel: ```cpp const int64_t token_idx = blockIdx.x; const scalar_t* row_in = input + token_idx * hidden_size; ``` For blockIdx.x = 0 to 15, row_in points to rows 0 to 15. Correct. ```cpp float thread_max = 0.0f; for (int i = tid; i < hidden_size; i += stride) { float v = fabsf(static_cast<float>(row_in[i])); thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, v); } ``` tid from 0 to 255. For tid=0: i=0, 256, 512, ..., 3840. For tid=255: i=255, 511, ..., 4095. Each thread processes 16 elements. Wait, there are 4096 elements and 256 threads. 4096/256 = 16 elements per thread. Correct. Reduction: ```cpp __shared__ float smem[256]; smem[tid] = thread_max; __syncthreads(); for (int s = blockDim.x / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) { if (tid < s) { smem[tid] = fmaxf(smem[tid], smem[tid + s]); } __syncthreads(); } ``` This does tree reduction. Smem starts with all 256 thread_max values. After first iter (s=128): smem[0..127] = max(smem[0..127], smem[128..255]). After s=64: smem[0..63] = max(smem[0..63], smem[64..127]). And so on. After s=1: smem[0] is the global max. Then: ```cpp __shared__ float absmax; if (tid == 0) { absmax = smem[0]; scale_out[token_idx] = absmax / 127.0f; } ``` Only tid=0 writes. Other threads don't write.
